COVID-19 Analysis: Cases, Deaths, Fatality Rate and Vaccination
Goal
Analyze COVID-19 trends in India, the United States, Brazil, and the United Kingdom using the Our World in Data (OWID) dataset.
This analysis focuses on:
- reported COVID-19 cases and deaths,
- a more stable smoothed daily case fatality rate (CFR),
- changes before and after the vaccine rollout period, and
- the relationship between vaccination coverage and CFR.
Important: This is an observational analysis. A decline in CFR over time cannot by itself prove that vaccination caused the decline.

import seaborn as sns
import plotly.express as px
import plotly.graph_objects as go
import warnings

warnings.filterwarnings('ignore')

URL = "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/owid/covid-19-data/master/public/data/owid-covid-data.csv"

print("Downloading OWID COVID-19 dataset...")
df = pd.read_csv(URL)
df['date'] = pd.to_datetime(df['date'])

print(f"Dataset: {df.shape[0]:,} rows x {df.shape[1]} columns")
print(f"Date range: {df['date'].min().date()} to {df['date'].max().date()}")

1. Data Preparation
Cell 4 of 23

1. Data Preparation
The OWID COVID-19 dataset is loaded once and the four countries are selected. Dates are converted to a standard datetime format and the numerical fields used in the analysis are cleaned.
The analysis uses OWID's smoothed daily cases and deaths rather than raw daily reports where possible. Smoothing reduces the effect of one-day reporting irregularities.


Code cell 5
Cell 5 of 23
Run 30
countries = ['India', 'United States', 'Brazil', 'United Kingdom']

d = df[df['location'].isin(countries)].copy()
d = d.sort_values(['location', 'date'])

# Ensure numeric fields are numeric
for col in ['new_cases', 'new_deaths', 'new_cases_smoothed', 'new_deaths_smoothed',
            'total_cases', 'total_deaths', 'people_fully_vaccinated_per_hundred']:
    if col in d.columns:
        d[col] = pd.to_numeric(d[col], errors='coerce')

print(f"Selected rows: {len(d):,}")
print("Countries:", d['location'].unique().tolist())
print("\nRows by country:")
print(d.groupby('location').size())

2. Method and Comparison Periods
Cell 6 of 23

2. Method and Comparison Periods
To avoid misleading early-pandemic values, the comparison uses the median daily CFR for two defined periods:
- Before: June 1, 2020 – December 31, 2020
- After: June 1, 2021 – June 30, 2023
Daily CFR is calculated as:
Smoothed daily deaths ÷ smoothed daily cases × 100
The median is used instead of the mean because it is less sensitive to unusually high or low reporting days.


Code cell 7
Cell 7 of 23
Run 31
# Smoothed daily case fatality rate
d['daily_cfr'] = np.where(
    d['new_cases_smoothed'] > 0,
    d['new_deaths_smoothed'] / d['new_cases_smoothed'] * 100,
    np.nan
)

pre = d[(d['date'] >= '2020-06-01') & (d['date'] < '2021-01-01')].copy()
post = d[(d['date'] >= '2021-06-01') & (d['date'] <= '2023-06-30')].copy()

summary = pd.DataFrame({
    'Before': pre.groupby('location')['daily_cfr'].median(),
    'After': post.groupby('location')['daily_cfr'].median()
}).round(2)
summary['Change (%)'] = ((summary['After'] - summary['Before']) / summary['Before'] * 100).round(1)

print("Median daily CFR (%)")
display(summary.loc[countries])

Median daily CFR (%)

3. COVID-19 Case Trends
Cell 8 of 23

3. COVID-19 Case Trends
This chart uses OWID's new_cases_smoothed field. It is preferable to interpreting raw one-day reporting spikes as genuine daily peaks.
Some large reported spikes can reflect delayed or backlogged reporting rather than a sudden change in infections.


Code cell 9
Cell 9 of 23
Run 32
fig = px.line(
    d, x='date', y='new_cases_smoothed', color='location',
    title='COVID-19 New Cases Over Time (OWID Smoothed)',
    labels={'new_cases_smoothed': 'Smoothed new cases per day', 'date': 'Date', 'location': 'Country'},
    template='plotly_white'
)
fig.update_layout(height=520)
fig.show()

4. Total Reported Deaths
Cell 10 of 23

4. Total Reported Deaths
This chart compares the maximum cumulative reported deaths recorded in the dataset for each country.
These are reported deaths, not an estimate of excess mortality, so differences in testing, death certification, and reporting practices should be considered.


Code cell 11
Cell 11 of 23
Run 33
deaths = (
    d.groupby('location', as_index=False)['total_deaths']
     .max()
     .sort_values('total_deaths')
)

fig = px.bar(
    deaths, x='total_deaths', y='location', orientation='h',
    title='Total Reported COVID-19 Deaths',
    labels={'total_deaths': 'Total deaths', 'location': 'Country'},
    template='plotly_white',
    text='total_deaths'
)
fig.update_traces(texttemplate='%{text:,.0f}', textposition='outside')
fig.update_layout(height=450)
fig.show()

print(deaths.to_string(index=False))

5. Monthly Case Heatmap
Cell 12 of 23

5. Monthly Case Heatmap
The heatmap covers January 2020 through December 2022, when reporting was substantially more informative than the later period.
Monthly totals are calculated from reported daily cases. Because reporting practices changed over time, the heatmap should be interpreted as a view of reported case activity rather than a perfect measure of actual infections.


Code cell 13
Cell 13 of 23
Run 34
d['month'] = d['date'].dt.to_period('M').astype(str)
monthly = (
    d.groupby(['location', 'month'])['new_cases']
     .sum(min_count=1)
     .reset_index()
)

pivot = monthly.pivot(index='location', columns='month', values='new_cases')
pivot = pivot.loc[:, '2020-01':'2022-12']

plt.figure(figsize=(16, 4.5))
sns.heatmap(pivot / 1e6, cmap='YlOrRd', linewidths=0.2,
            cbar_kws={'label': 'Reported cases (millions)'})
plt.title('Monthly Reported COVID-19 Cases: Jan 2020–Dec 2022')
plt.xlabel('Month')
plt.ylabel('Country')
plt.tight_layout()
plt.show()




6. Before vs After: Median Daily CFR
Cell 14 of 23

6. Before vs After: Median Daily CFR
The comparison below shows the median smoothed daily CFR for the two defined periods.
A decrease appears in all four countries. However, the change may reflect several factors, including vaccination, prior immunity, treatments, variants, testing practices, and changes in reporting—not vaccination alone.


Code cell 15
Cell 15 of 23
Run 35
plot_df = summary.loc[countries, ['Before', 'After']].reset_index().melt(
    id_vars='location', var_name='Period', value_name='Median CFR (%)'
)

fig = px.bar(
    plot_df, x='location', y='Median CFR (%)', color='Period', barmode='group',
    text='Median CFR (%)',
    title='Median Daily Case Fatality Rate: Before vs After',
    labels={'location': 'Country'},
    template='plotly_white'
)
fig.update_traces(texttemplate='%{text:.2f}%', textposition='outside')
fig.update_layout(height=500)
fig.show()

7. Daily CFR Over Time
Cell 16 of 23

7. Daily CFR Over Time
The time-series view shows how the smoothed daily CFR changed during the pandemic. The dashed line marks January 1, 2021 as a visual reference point; the formal before/after comparison above uses the June 2020 and June 2021 periods defined in the methodology.


Code cell 17
Cell 17 of 23
Run 36
fig = px.line(
    d[d['date'] >= '2020-06-01'],
    x='date', y='daily_cfr', color='location',
    title='Smoothed Daily Case Fatality Rate Over Time',
    labels={'daily_cfr': 'Daily CFR (%)', 'date': 'Date', 'location': 'Country'},
    template='plotly_white'
)
fig.add_vline(x=pd.Timestamp('2021-01-01'), line_dash='dash', line_color='gray')
fig.update_yaxes(rangemode='tozero')
fig.update_layout(height=520)
fig.show()

8. Vaccination Coverage vs CFR
Cell 18 of 23

8. Vaccination Coverage vs CFR
This scatter plot explores whether higher full-vaccination coverage tends to coincide with a lower smoothed daily CFR.
A negative relationship can be useful as a descriptive finding, but correlation does not establish causation. Both variables change over time and may be affected by other factors.


Code cell 19
Cell 19 of 23
Run 37
scatter_df = d[['location', 'date', 'people_fully_vaccinated_per_hundred', 'daily_cfr']].dropna().copy()

fig = px.scatter(
    scatter_df,
    x='people_fully_vaccinated_per_hundred',
    y='daily_cfr',
    color='location',
    hover_data=['date'],
    opacity=0.45,
    title='Vaccination Coverage vs Smoothed Daily CFR',
    labels={
        'people_fully_vaccinated_per_hundred': 'People fully vaccinated (%)',
        'daily_cfr': 'Daily CFR (%)',
        'location': 'Country'
    },
    template='plotly_white'
)
fig.update_layout(height=520)
fig.show()

            observer.disconnect();
        }}
}});

// Listen for the removal of the full notebook cells
var notebookContainer = gd.closest('#notebook-container');
if (notebookContainer) {{
    x.observe(notebookContainer, {childList: true});
}}

// Listen for the clearing of the current output cell
var outputEl = gd.closest('.output');
if (outputEl) {{
    x.observe(outputEl, {childList: true});
}}

                        })                };                            </script>        </div>
</body>
</html>



Code cell 20
Cell 20 of 23

# Pearson correlation between full vaccination coverage and smoothed daily CFR
correlation = (
    scatter_df.groupby('location')
    .apply(lambda g: g['people_fully_vaccinated_per_hundred'].corr(g['daily_cfr']), include_groups=False)
    .round(2)
    .rename('Pearson correlation')
)

print("Vaccination coverage vs daily CFR correlation:")
print(correlation)


Key Findings
Cell 21 of 23

Key Findings
- The four-country analysis contains substantial differences in reported COVID-19 cases and deaths.
- The median smoothed daily CFR fell after the vaccine rollout period in all four countries.
- Example changes: United States 1.57% → 0.93% and Brazil 2.42% → 0.83%.
- The case trend is presented using OWID's smoothed series because raw daily peaks can be dominated by reporting/backlog spikes.
- The vaccination-vs-CFR scatter analysis is descriptive: a negative relationship does not prove a causal vaccine effect.


Limitations
Cell 22 of 23

Limitations
1. Reporting gaps and changes: COVID-19 testing, case definitions, death reporting, and reporting frequency changed substantially over time.
2. Reporting spikes: Raw daily case counts can contain backlog or delayed-reporting spikes, so a single-day peak should not automatically be interpreted as a true infection peak.
3. Missing early values: Early CFR estimates can be unstable when case counts are small or reporting is incomplete. The analysis therefore uses a defined period and the median of smoothed daily values.
4. Confounding factors: Variants, prior infection, treatments, healthcare capacity, demographics, behavior, and testing can all influence CFR.
5. Correlation is not causation: The vaccination/CFR relationship is observational and cannot establish that vaccination alone caused the decline.
6. Reported deaths are not excess deaths: The cumulative death comparison reflects reported COVID-19 deaths in OWID and does not estimate total pandemic mortality.


Conclusion
Cell 23 of 23

Conclusion
The analysis shows a clear decline in median smoothed daily case fatality rate across India, the United States, Brazil, and the United Kingdom when comparing the defined pre- and post-rollout periods.
The results are consistent with improved outcomes over time, but the notebook does not claim that vaccination alone caused the decline. Changes in variants, immunity, treatment, healthcare, testing, and reporting also matter.
Overall, the project demonstrates a complete data-analysis workflow: data collection → cleaning → metric engineering → visualization → comparison → interpretation → limitations.
